=== chapter p5-meshes
title: Procedural meshes
blurb: Indexed triangle meshes in typed arrays sized upfront: grids, UV and icospheres, tubes with parallel-transport frames, lathes and extrusions, smooth and flat normals, noise displacement, marching squares and a marching cubes table you derive yourself, OBJ export, and a z-buffered software renderer to look at it all.
--- body
## What a mesh is to the GPU

Every 3D model a GPU draws is, in the end, a few flat arrays:

| Array | Type | Per | Holds |
|---|---|---|---|
| positions | `Float32Array` | vertex | x, y, z |
| normals | `Float32Array` | vertex | unit surface normal, for lighting |
| uvs | `Float32Array` | vertex | u, v texture coordinates |
| indices | `Uint32Array` (or `Uint16Array` under 65,536 vertices) | triangle corner | which vertex each corner uses |

An **indexed** mesh stores each vertex once and refers to it from every triangle that touches it. A closed mesh has about twice as many triangles as vertices and each vertex is shared by about six triangles, so indexing stores a vertex (32 bytes here) once instead of six times, and the GPU's post-transform cache runs the vertex shader once for a shared vertex instead of per corner. These arrays upload to `GPUBuffer`s unchanged in the WebGPU course: that's why they are typed arrays from the start, and why this chapter never builds meshes as arrays of `{x, y, z}` objects.

**Winding.** A triangle's *front* is the side from which its corners appear counterclockwise. Its geometric normal is `(p1 − p0) × (p2 − p0)`, pointing out of the front. Renderers skip back faces (half the triangles of a closed mesh, for free), so every generator in this chapter must produce outward, counterclockwise triangles; the tests check it.

**Sizes upfront.** Almost every procedural mesh has a vertex and triangle count you can compute from its parameters: a grid of nx × nz cells has (nx+1)(nz+1) vertices and 2·nx·nz triangles. Compute them, allocate each array once at its final size, and fill it with an index you advance. Growing plain arrays with `push` and converting at the end allocates several times over and keeps every number boxed until the end.

```js
// Memory of a 256x256-cell grid, indexed vs a flat triangle soup, and how fast it fills.
const nx = 256, nz = 256, nv = (nx + 1) * (nz + 1), nt = nx * nz * 2;
const bytesPerVertex = (3 + 3 + 2) * 4;
console.log('vertices', nv, 'triangles', nt);
console.log('indexed:', ((nv * bytesPerVertex + nt * 3 * 4) / 1e6).toFixed(2), 'MB');
console.log('soup:   ', ((nt * 3 * bytesPerVertex) / 1e6).toFixed(2), 'MB');

let t = performance.now();
const pos = new Float32Array(nv * 3), idx = new Uint32Array(nt * 3);
for (let j = 0, v = 0; j <= nz; j++) for (let i = 0; i <= nx; i++, v++) { pos[v * 3] = i; pos[v * 3 + 2] = j; }
for (let j = 0, k = 0; j < nz; j++) for (let i = 0; i < nx; i++) {
  const a = j * (nx + 1) + i, b = a + 1, c = a + nx + 1, d = c + 1;
  idx[k++] = a; idx[k++] = c; idx[k++] = b; idx[k++] = b; idx[k++] = c; idx[k++] = d;
}
console.log('typed arrays, sized upfront:', (performance.now() - t).toFixed(1), 'ms');

t = performance.now();
const verts = [], tris = [];
for (let j = 0; j <= nz; j++) for (let i = 0; i <= nx; i++) verts.push({ x: i, y: 0, z: j });
for (let j = 0; j < nz; j++) for (let i = 0; i < nx; i++) {
  const a = j * (nx + 1) + i;
  tris.push([a, a + nx + 1, a + 1], [a + 1, a + nx + 1, a + nx + 2]);
}
const packed = new Float32Array(verts.length * 3);
verts.forEach((v, i) => { packed[i * 3] = v.x; packed[i * 3 + 1] = v.y; packed[i * 3 + 2] = v.z; });
console.log('objects, then packed:       ', (performance.now() - t).toFixed(1), 'ms');
```

## Normals: smooth and flat

Lighting needs a normal per vertex. For a smooth surface, average the normals of the triangles around the vertex. The trick (from the classic "area-weighted normals" observation): the cross product `(p1 − p0) × (p2 − p0)` has length equal to twice the triangle's area, so if you *don't* normalize it before summing, big triangles automatically count more than slivers. Zero the normal array, add each triangle's raw cross product to its three corners, then normalize every vertex once. That's one pass over the triangles and one over the vertices: O(V + T). The naive "for each vertex, find the triangles that use it" is O(V × T) and a 100k-vertex mesh takes minutes.

Weighting by area is cheap and good; weighting by the corner *angle* (Thürrner and Wüthrich 1998) is a bit better on irregular meshes and costs an `acos` per corner. Both beat the plain average, which lets one sliver triangle tilt the normal.

For hard edges (a cube, a crystal, low-poly style) you want **flat** shading: each triangle shows its own face normal. A vertex can only have one normal, so flat shading duplicates vertices: every triangle gets its own three. The same applies to any discontinuity: a UV seam or a color change needs split vertices too. That's the main thing to unlearn from modelling tools: a "vertex" in a GPU mesh is a unique combination of *all* its attributes.

> **Performance:** in hot loops over meshes, read and write the flat arrays with computed offsets (`P[a * 3 + 1]`) and keep vectors in local scalars (`const ux = ..., uy = ..., uz = ...`). A helper that returns `{ x, y, z }` or `[x, y, z]` per call allocates per vertex; V8 sometimes optimizes it away, often doesn't, and you can't see which from the source.

## Two spheres

A **UV sphere** sweeps rings of latitude (θ from 0 at the north pole to π) and segments of longitude (φ from 0 to 2π): `p = r (sin θ cos φ, cos θ, sin θ sin φ)`. Its UVs are trivially `(i/seg, j/rings)`, which is why it's the default for globes. Its flaws: the triangles bunch up at the poles (tiny slivers there, big quads at the equator), the ring at each pole collapses to a point (the triangles touching it would be degenerate, so you skip them), and the seam at φ = 2π needs a duplicated column of vertices so u can go from 1 back to 0.

An **icosphere** starts from the icosahedron (12 vertices, 20 equilateral triangles) and subdivides: split each triangle into four through its edge midpoints, push the midpoints out to the sphere, repeat. After n levels it has 20·4ⁿ triangles and 10·4ⁿ + 2 vertices, all nearly the same size. That uniformity is what you want for displacement (planets, asteroids, rocks): noise sampled at evenly spaced vertices looks the same everywhere, without pole pinching.

The one subtle part is that neighboring triangles share an edge, and both create its midpoint. Without care you get two vertices at the same place and a cracked mesh. Keep a cache from edge to midpoint index, keyed by the **ordered pair** of endpoint indices (smaller first). Make the key a single number, `min * nv + max`, rather than a string like `` `${a},${b}` ``: it's exact (well under 2⁵³), hashes faster and allocates nothing.

```js
// Wireframes: UV sphere (left) bunches at the poles; icosphere (right) is uniform.
const ctx = canvas.getContext('2d'), W = canvas.width;
ctx.fillStyle = '#0f1218'; ctx.fillRect(0, 0, W, W);
const rotX = 0.5, cs = Math.cos(rotX), sn = Math.sin(rotX);
const proj = (x, y, z, cx) => { const y2 = y * cs - z * sn, z2 = y * sn + z * cs; return [cx + x * W * 0.2, W / 2 - y2 * W * 0.2, z2]; };
function wire(P, I, cx, color) {
  ctx.strokeStyle = color; ctx.beginPath();
  for (let t = 0; t < I.length; t += 3) for (let k = 0; k < 3; k++) {
    const a = I[t + k] * 3, b = I[t + (k + 1) % 3] * 3;
    const pa = proj(P[a], P[a + 1], P[a + 2], cx), pb = proj(P[b], P[b + 1], P[b + 2], cx);
    if (pa[2] + pb[2] < 0) continue;                       // back half: skip
    ctx.moveTo(pa[0], pa[1]); ctx.lineTo(pb[0], pb[1]);
  }
  ctx.stroke();
}
// UV sphere
const seg = 24, rings = 16, U = [], UI = [];
for (let j = 0; j <= rings; j++) for (let i = 0; i <= seg; i++) {
  const th = Math.PI * j / rings, ph = 2 * Math.PI * i / seg;
  U.push(Math.sin(th) * Math.cos(ph), Math.cos(th), Math.sin(th) * Math.sin(ph));
}
for (let j = 0; j < rings; j++) for (let i = 0; i < seg; i++) {
  const a = j * (seg + 1) + i, b = a + 1, c = a + seg + 1, d = c + 1;
  if (j) UI.push(a, b, c);
  if (j < rings - 1) UI.push(b, d, c);
}
wire(U, UI, W * 0.27, '#ffb36b');
// Icosphere, 3 levels
const t = (1 + Math.sqrt(5)) / 2;
const P = [-1, t, 0, 1, t, 0, -1, -t, 0, 1, -t, 0, 0, -1, t, 0, 1, t, 0, -1, -t, 0, 1, -t, t, 0, -1, t, 0, 1, -t, 0, -1, -t, 0, 1];
let F = [0, 11, 5, 0, 5, 1, 0, 1, 7, 0, 7, 10, 0, 10, 11, 1, 5, 9, 5, 11, 4, 11, 10, 2, 10, 7, 6, 7, 1, 8, 3, 9, 4, 3, 4, 2, 3, 2, 6, 3, 6, 8, 3, 8, 9, 4, 9, 5, 2, 4, 11, 6, 2, 10, 8, 6, 7, 9, 8, 1];
for (let i = 0; i < P.length; i += 3) { const l = Math.hypot(P[i], P[i + 1], P[i + 2]); P[i] /= l; P[i + 1] /= l; P[i + 2] /= l; }
for (let s = 0; s < 3; s++) {
  const cache = new Map(), out = [];
  const mid = (a, b) => {
    const key = a < b ? a * 100000 + b : b * 100000 + a;
    if (!cache.has(key)) {
      const x = P[a * 3] + P[b * 3], y = P[a * 3 + 1] + P[b * 3 + 1], z = P[a * 3 + 2] + P[b * 3 + 2], l = Math.hypot(x, y, z);
      cache.set(key, P.length / 3); P.push(x / l, y / l, z / l);
    }
    return cache.get(key);
  };
  for (let i = 0; i < F.length; i += 3) {
    const a = F[i], b = F[i + 1], c = F[i + 2], ab = mid(a, b), bc = mid(b, c), ca = mid(c, a);
    out.push(a, ab, ca, b, bc, ab, c, ca, bc, ab, bc, ca);
  }
  F = out;
}
wire(P, F, W * 0.73, '#7fd4ff');
console.log('uv sphere', U.length / 3, 'vertices', UI.length / 3, 'triangles; icosphere', P.length / 3, 'vertices', F.length / 3, 'triangles');
```

## Tubes along paths: parallel transport

To turn the L-system's branch segments (or a road, a cable, a tentacle) into a mesh, sweep a circle along a path. At each path point you need a frame: the tangent T, and two vectors N and B spanning the plane of the circle.

The textbook choice, the **Frenet frame** (N = direction of curvature), fails in practice: on a straight piece the curvature is zero and N is undefined, and at an inflection point it flips 180° and the tube twists into a bow tie. The fix is the **rotation-minimizing** or **parallel-transport** frame (Bishop 1975; Wang et al. 2008 for the "double reflection" refinement): choose any N perpendicular to the first tangent, then carry it along, at each point rotating it only as much as needed to stay perpendicular to the new tangent. The cheap version for densely sampled paths projects the previous N onto the plane perpendicular to the new tangent and renormalizes:

```text
N_i = normalize(N_{i-1} − T_i (T_i · N_{i-1}))       B_i = T_i × N_i
```

A planar curve then keeps B pointing out of its plane all along, with no flips at inflections. Closed loops (a torus knot) end with a small twist mismatch; distribute that angle over the length if you need a perfect closure. For L-system trees, generate one tube per branch (the parameters in the turtle's segment list give radius per point) and merge them.

```js
// Frenet vs parallel transport on an S-curve in the xy plane: Frenet's normal flips at the
// inflection (and is NaN exactly there, where the curvature is zero).
const n = 9, pts = [];
for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) { const x = i / (n - 1) * 2 - 1; pts.push([x, Math.sin(x * Math.PI) * 0.5, 0]); }
const sub = (a, b) => [a[0] - b[0], a[1] - b[1], a[2] - b[2]], nrm = v => { const l = Math.hypot(...v); return v.map(c => c / l); };
const dot = (a, b) => a[0] * b[0] + a[1] * b[1] + a[2] * b[2];
const T = pts.map((p, i) => nrm(sub(pts[Math.min(i + 1, n - 1)], pts[Math.max(i - 1, 0)])));
let N = nrm([-T[0][1], T[0][0], 0]);                 // start: any vector perpendicular to T[0]
for (let i = 1; i < n - 1; i++) {
  const frenet = nrm(sub(sub(T[i + 1], T[i - 1]), T[i].map(c => c * dot(T[i], sub(T[i + 1], T[i - 1])))));
  N = nrm(sub(N, T[i].map(c => c * dot(T[i], N))));
  console.log(`x=${pts[i][0].toFixed(2)}  frenet N=(${frenet.map(c => c.toFixed(2)).join(', ')})  transported N=(${N.map(c => c.toFixed(2)).join(', ')})`);
}
```

## Lathes and extrusions

Two more generators cover a surprising share of man-made props:

- A **lathe** (surface of revolution) spins a 2D profile (radius, height) around the y axis: vases, bottles, columns, chess pieces, lamp posts, tree trunks with flare at the base. It's a UV sphere with an arbitrary profile instead of a semicircle: (profile points) × (segments + 1) vertices with a seam column.
- An **extrusion** pushes a 2D polygon along z: walls from floor plans, text, gears, cookie-cutter shapes. The side walls want hard edges, so each wall quad gets its own four vertices with a flat normal. The caps need the polygon triangulated: **ear clipping** (Meisters 1975) repeatedly cuts off an "ear", a convex corner whose triangle contains no other polygon vertex, until a triangle remains. It's O(n²) and handles any simple polygon, concave included; for big polygons or polygons with holes use a library like earcut (Mapbox), which is the same idea with a z-order spatial index.

## Displacement with noise

Procedural planets, rocks and terrain start as a smooth base mesh and move every vertex along its normal by a noise value: `p' = p + n · amp · fbm(p · freq)`. Sample the noise at the vertex's **3D position**, not its UV: positions are continuous across seams, so the two copies of a seam vertex move identically and the mesh stays closed, and there's no pole pinching. After moving the vertices, the old normals are wrong: recompute them. For a planet, clamp the displacement from below to get flat oceans (`max(h, seaLevel)`), and color by height in the shader.

> **Performance:** noise is by far the most expensive thing per vertex here (each fBm octave is a Perlin evaluation with 8 gradient lookups). On an indexed mesh you evaluate it once per vertex; on a triangle soup you'd evaluate the same point six times *and* get cracks wherever floating-point order differs. Later, do it on the GPU in a compute or vertex shader; the WebGPU course does.

## Isosurfaces: marching squares and marching cubes

A scalar field (a noise function, a signed distance function, a density grid from a sculpting tool or a fluid sim) defines a surface implicitly: where `f(p) = iso`. Caves, overhangs, metaballs and blobs can't be height fields; you extract them from a density grid.

**Marching squares** is the 2D version. For each grid cell, look at its 4 corners: which are inside (`f < iso`)? That's a 4-bit case number, 0 to 15. A 16-entry table says which cell edges the contour crosses and how to connect them. Each crossing point is placed by linear interpolation along the edge: `t = (iso − f_a) / (f_b − f_a)`. Cases 5 and 10 are **saddles** (two diagonal corners inside): the contour could connect either way. Decide with the average of the four corners (the value at the cell center): if the center is inside, the inside regions join through it.

```js
// Contour lines of an fBm height field by marching squares (compact version).
function noise2(seed) {           // value noise with smooth interpolation
  const h = (x, y) => { let n = Math.imul(x, 374761393) + Math.imul(y, 668265263) + seed | 0; n = Math.imul(n ^ (n >>> 13), 1274126177); return ((n ^ (n >>> 16)) >>> 0) / 4294967296; };
  return (x, y) => {
    const X = Math.floor(x), Y = Math.floor(y), fx = x - X, fy = y - Y, u = fx * fx * (3 - 2 * fx), v = fy * fy * (3 - 2 * fy);
    const a = h(X, Y), b = h(X + 1, Y), c = h(X, Y + 1), d = h(X + 1, Y + 1);
    return a + (b - a) * u + (c - a) * v + (a - b - c + d) * u * v;
  };
}
const N = 128, f = new Float32Array(N * N), nz = noise2(7);
for (let y = 0; y < N; y++) for (let x = 0; x < N; x++) {
  let s = 0, amp = 0.5, fr = 1 / 24;
  for (let o = 0; o < 5; o++) { s += amp * nz(x * fr, y * fr); amp *= 0.5; fr *= 2; }
  f[x + y * N] = s;
}
const TABLE = [[], [3, 0], [0, 1], [3, 1], [1, 2], [3, 0, 1, 2], [0, 2], [3, 2], [2, 3], [0, 2], [0, 1, 2, 3], [1, 2], [1, 3], [0, 1], [3, 0], []];
const ctx = canvas.getContext('2d'), W = canvas.width, sc = W / (N - 1);
const img = ctx.createImageData(W, W);
for (let py = 0; py < W; py++) for (let px = 0; px < W; px++) {
  const v = f[Math.min(N - 1, Math.round(px / sc)) + Math.min(N - 1, Math.round(py / sc)) * N], o = (py * W + px) * 4;
  img.data[o] = 40 + 120 * v; img.data[o + 1] = 70 + 140 * v; img.data[o + 2] = 60 + 60 * v; img.data[o + 3] = 255;
}
ctx.putImageData(img, 0, 0);
ctx.strokeStyle = 'rgba(20,20,10,0.8)'; ctx.beginPath();
const ex = [0, 0, 0, 0], ey = [0, 0, 0, 0];
for (let iso = 0.3; iso < 0.75; iso += 0.05) {
  for (let y = 0; y < N - 1; y++) for (let x = 0; x < N - 1; x++) {
    const i = x + y * N, v0 = f[i], v1 = f[i + 1], v2 = f[i + 1 + N], v3 = f[i + N];
    const c = (v0 < iso) | (v1 < iso) << 1 | (v2 < iso) << 2 | (v3 < iso) << 3;
    if (c === 0 || c === 15) continue;
    ex[0] = x + (iso - v0) / (v1 - v0); ey[0] = y; ex[1] = x + 1; ey[1] = y + (iso - v1) / (v2 - v1);
    ex[2] = x + 1 - (iso - v2) / (v3 - v2); ey[2] = y + 1; ex[3] = x; ey[3] = y + 1 - (iso - v3) / (v0 - v3);
    let t = TABLE[c];
    if ((c === 5 || c === 10) && (v0 + v1 + v2 + v3) / 4 < iso) t = TABLE[15 - c];
    for (let j = 0; j < t.length; j += 2) { ctx.moveTo(ex[t[j]] * sc, ey[t[j]] * sc); ctx.lineTo(ex[t[j + 1]] * sc, ey[t[j + 1]] * sc); }
  }
}
ctx.stroke();
```

**Marching cubes** (Lorensen and Cline, SIGGRAPH 1987) does the same per cube of 8 samples: an 8-bit case (256 cases), a table listing up to 5 triangles per case as triples of the 12 cube edges, and vertices interpolated along the crossed edges. Everyone copies Paul Bourke's 256-row table from his 1994 page "Polygonising a scalar field". You'll **derive** the table instead, which takes 30 lines and teaches why it works:

1. For a case, walk around each of the cube's 6 faces (counterclockwise seen from outside). Along the way the inside/outside state flips at every crossed edge, so crossings alternate *leaving* (inside → outside) and *entering*.
2. Connect each leaving crossing to the entering crossing just before it. That draws the contour segment that cuts off the inside corners between them. On an ambiguous face (two diagonal corners inside), this rule cuts off each inside corner separately. Neighboring cubes see the same face with the same corners and make the same choice, so the surface has no holes; the original 1987 table famously did *not* guarantee that.
3. Every crossed edge is shared by two faces: it starts a segment in one and ends a segment in the other. Follow the segments from edge to edge until you're back where you started: a closed loop, a polygon on the surface.
4. Fan-triangulate each loop, ordered so the triangles face outside.

Two practical points make marching cubes fast and correct. First, **share vertices**: a crossed grid edge is shared by up to four cubes, and each would create its own vertex. Store the vertex index created for each (grid point, axis) in an `Int32Array` and look it up; the mesh is then watertight and indexed, and smooth normals work. Second, **count first**: one pass counts crossed edges (= vertices) and table triangles, so every array is allocated once at its exact size.

Marching cubes has relatives you'll meet: **dual contouring** (Ju et al. 2002) keeps sharp features, **surface nets** places one vertex per cell for smoother, more even triangles, and **transvoxel** (Lengyel 2010) stitches levels of detail for voxel terrain.

## Seeing it: a software renderer

Rather than waiting for the WebGPU course, you'll write a small rasterizer that does what the GPU's fixed-function hardware does, on the CPU, into an `ImageData`:

1. **Transform each vertex once**: multiply by the view-projection matrix to get clip coordinates, divide by w, map to pixels. Store the results in a scratch typed array indexed by vertex: per-vertex work is never repeated per triangle.
2. **Cull back faces** by the sign of the screen-space area.
3. **Rasterize** each triangle over its bounding box (clamped to the screen) with **edge functions**: for a pixel center p, the three signed areas of (edge, p) are its barycentric coordinates times the triangle area. All three non-negative means inside (Pineda 1988; this is how GPUs do it, in parallel over tiles).
4. **Z-buffer**: interpolate depth, keep the pixel only if it's nearer than what the depth buffer holds (Catmull 1974). Unlike the painter's algorithm (sort triangles back to front, draw in order), it's order-independent and right for intersecting triangles, at the cost of a float per pixel.
5. **Shade**: Lambert's cosine law, intensity = ambient + (1 − ambient) · max(0, n · l), computed per vertex and interpolated (Gouraud shading, 1971). Per-pixel normals (Phong) look better on coarse meshes and cost more per pixel.

> **Performance:** the bounding box is the difference between a usable rasterizer and an unusable one: scanning every pixel of the screen for every triangle costs W·H per triangle, while a typical small triangle covers a few dozen pixels. Everything per pixel works on local numbers and typed arrays; there are no objects, closures or array allocations inside the pixel loop.

## Exporting: OBJ

Wavefront OBJ is the plain-text format every tool imports: `v x y z`, `vt u v`, `vn x y z` lines, then faces `f a/at/an b/bt/bn c/ct/cn` with **1-based** indices. Since our arrays share one index per vertex, all three indices are the same. Build the file as an array of lines and `join` once, then hand it to a `Blob` for download.

## The project

The exercises grow one mesh library, ending with a scene containing every generator, rendered and turning on the canvas by your own rasterizer:

1. Mesh storage and a grid.
2. Smooth and flat normals.
3. The z-buffered rasterizer.
4. UV sphere.
5. Icosphere with an edge-midpoint cache.
6. Noise displacement.
7. Tubes with parallel-transport frames.
8. Lathe and extrusion with ear clipping.
9. Merging and OBJ export.
10. Marching squares.
11. Deriving the marching cubes table.
12. Marching cubes, and the final scene.
=== exercise mesh-grid
title: 1. Mesh storage and a grid
--- prompt
Start the mesh library. The starter brings in `mulberry32`, `makePerlin3(seed)` (improved Perlin noise, returns roughly −1..1) and `fbm3(noise, x, y, z, octaves, lacunarity, gain)` from the noise chapter.

Write:

- `createMesh(nv, nt)` returning `{ positions, normals, uvs, indices }`: `Float32Array(nv * 3)`, `Float32Array(nv * 3)`, `Float32Array(nv * 2)`, `Uint32Array(nt * 3)`, all zero.
- `gridMesh(w, d, nx, nz)`: a flat grid in the XZ plane at y = 0, `w` wide (x) and `d` deep (z), centered on the origin, with `nx × nz` cells.

Layout (the tests check it exactly):

- Vertex `v = j * (nx + 1) + i` for `i = 0..nx`, `j = 0..nz` sits at `x = −w/2 + w·i/nx`, `z = −d/2 + d·j/nz`, with normal (0, 1, 0) and uv `(i/nx, j/nz)`.
- Cell (i, j) with corners `a = j*(nx+1)+i`, `b = a+1`, `c = a+nx+1`, `e = c+1` makes the triangles `(a, c, b)` and `(b, c, e)`, cells in row order. Both face up (+y): check it with the cross product.

Compute the counts first and fill the arrays with running indices; no `push`.
--- starter
// ---- From earlier chapters: seeded PRNG and 3D Perlin noise + fBm (p2-noise) ----
function mulberry32(seed) {
  let a = seed >>> 0;
  return function () {
    a = (a + 0x6D2B79F5) >>> 0;
    let t = a;
    t = Math.imul(t ^ (t >>> 15), t | 1);
    t ^= t + Math.imul(t ^ (t >>> 7), t | 61);
    return ((t ^ (t >>> 14)) >>> 0) / 4294967296;
  };
}
// Improved Perlin noise (Perlin 2002) with a seeded permutation table. Returns roughly [-1, 1].
function makePerlin3(seed) {
  const rng = mulberry32(seed), p = new Uint8Array(512), perm = new Uint8Array(256);
  for (let i = 0; i < 256; i++) perm[i] = i;
  for (let i = 255; i > 0; i--) { const j = (rng() * (i + 1)) | 0, t = perm[i]; perm[i] = perm[j]; perm[j] = t; }
  for (let i = 0; i < 512; i++) p[i] = perm[i & 255];
  const fade = t => t * t * t * (t * (t * 6 - 15) + 10);
  const lerp = (a, b, t) => a + t * (b - a);
  const grad = (h, x, y, z) => {
    h &= 15;
    const u = h < 8 ? x : y, v = h < 4 ? y : (h === 12 || h === 14 ? x : z);
    return ((h & 1) ? -u : u) + ((h & 2) ? -v : v);
  };
  return function (x, y, z) {
    const X = Math.floor(x), Y = Math.floor(y), Z = Math.floor(z);
    x -= X; y -= Y; z -= Z;
    const xi = X & 255, yi = Y & 255, zi = Z & 255;
    const u = fade(x), v = fade(y), w = fade(z);
    const A = p[xi] + yi, AA = p[A] + zi, AB = p[A + 1] + zi, B = p[xi + 1] + yi, BA = p[B] + zi, BB = p[B + 1] + zi;
    return lerp(
      lerp(lerp(grad(p[AA], x, y, z), grad(p[BA], x - 1, y, z), u), lerp(grad(p[AB], x, y - 1, z), grad(p[BB], x - 1, y - 1, z), u), v),
      lerp(lerp(grad(p[AA + 1], x, y, z - 1), grad(p[BA + 1], x - 1, y, z - 1), u), lerp(grad(p[AB + 1], x, y - 1, z - 1), grad(p[BB + 1], x - 1, y - 1, z - 1), u), v),
      w);
  };
}
function fbm3(noise, x, y, z, octaves = 5, lacunarity = 2, gain = 0.5) {
  let sum = 0, amp = 1, freq = 1;
  for (let o = 0; o < octaves; o++) {
    sum += amp * noise(x * freq, y * freq, z * freq);
    freq *= lacunarity; amp *= gain;
  }
  return sum;
}

function createMesh(nv, nt) {
  return { positions: [], normals: [], uvs: [], indices: [] };
}

function gridMesh(w, d, nx, nz) {
  return createMesh(0, 0);
}
--- solution
// ---- From earlier chapters: seeded PRNG and 3D Perlin noise + fBm (p2-noise) ----
function mulberry32(seed) {
  let a = seed >>> 0;
  return function () {
    a = (a + 0x6D2B79F5) >>> 0;
    let t = a;
    t = Math.imul(t ^ (t >>> 15), t | 1);
    t ^= t + Math.imul(t ^ (t >>> 7), t | 61);
    return ((t ^ (t >>> 14)) >>> 0) / 4294967296;
  };
}
// Improved Perlin noise (Perlin 2002) with a seeded permutation table. Returns roughly [-1, 1].
function makePerlin3(seed) {
  const rng = mulberry32(seed), p = new Uint8Array(512), perm = new Uint8Array(256);
  for (let i = 0; i < 256; i++) perm[i] = i;
  for (let i = 255; i > 0; i--) { const j = (rng() * (i + 1)) | 0, t = perm[i]; perm[i] = perm[j]; perm[j] = t; }
  for (let i = 0; i < 512; i++) p[i] = perm[i & 255];
  const fade = t => t * t * t * (t * (t * 6 - 15) + 10);
  const lerp = (a, b, t) => a + t * (b - a);
  const grad = (h, x, y, z) => {
    h &= 15;
    const u = h < 8 ? x : y, v = h < 4 ? y : (h === 12 || h === 14 ? x : z);
    return ((h & 1) ? -u : u) + ((h & 2) ? -v : v);
  };
  return function (x, y, z) {
    const X = Math.floor(x), Y = Math.floor(y), Z = Math.floor(z);
    x -= X; y -= Y; z -= Z;
    const xi = X & 255, yi = Y & 255, zi = Z & 255;
    const u = fade(x), v = fade(y), w = fade(z);
    const A = p[xi] + yi, AA = p[A] + zi, AB = p[A + 1] + zi, B = p[xi + 1] + yi, BA = p[B] + zi, BB = p[B + 1] + zi;
    return lerp(
      lerp(lerp(grad(p[AA], x, y, z), grad(p[BA], x - 1, y, z), u), lerp(grad(p[AB], x, y - 1, z), grad(p[BB], x - 1, y - 1, z), u), v),
      lerp(lerp(grad(p[AA + 1], x, y, z - 1), grad(p[BA + 1], x - 1, y, z - 1), u), lerp(grad(p[AB + 1], x, y - 1, z - 1), grad(p[BB + 1], x - 1, y - 1, z - 1), u), v),
      w);
  };
}
function fbm3(noise, x, y, z, octaves = 5, lacunarity = 2, gain = 0.5) {
  let sum = 0, amp = 1, freq = 1;
  for (let o = 0; o < octaves; o++) {
    sum += amp * noise(x * freq, y * freq, z * freq);
    freq *= lacunarity; amp *= gain;
  }
  return sum;
}

// Indexed triangle mesh: every array allocated once at its final size.
function createMesh(nv, nt) {
  return {
    positions: new Float32Array(nv * 3),
    normals: new Float32Array(nv * 3),
    uvs: new Float32Array(nv * 2),
    indices: new Uint32Array(nt * 3),
  };
}

function gridMesh(w, d, nx, nz) {
  const m = createMesh((nx + 1) * (nz + 1), nx * nz * 2);
  const { positions: P, normals: N, uvs: U, indices: I } = m;
  for (let j = 0, v = 0; j <= nz; j++) {
    for (let i = 0; i <= nx; i++, v++) {
      P[v * 3] = -w / 2 + w * i / nx; P[v * 3 + 1] = 0; P[v * 3 + 2] = -d / 2 + d * j / nz;
      N[v * 3 + 1] = 1;
      U[v * 2] = i / nx; U[v * 2 + 1] = j / nz;
    }
  }
  for (let j = 0, k = 0; j < nz; j++) {
    for (let i = 0; i < nx; i++) {
      const a = j * (nx + 1) + i, b = a + 1, c = a + nx + 1, e = c + 1;
      I[k++] = a; I[k++] = c; I[k++] = b;
      I[k++] = b; I[k++] = c; I[k++] = e;
    }
  }
  return m;
}
--- hint
Two nested loops for the vertices with a running `v`, two for the cells with a running write index `k`.
--- hint
Check the winding: for `a = (0,0,0)`, `c = (0,0,1)`, `b = (1,0,0)`, `(c − a) × (b − a) = (0,0,1) × (1,0,0) = (0,1,0)`.
--- harness
{
  const m0 = createMesh(5, 2);
  check(m0.positions instanceof Float32Array && m0.positions.length === 15, 'positions: Float32Array(nv * 3)');
  check(m0.normals instanceof Float32Array && m0.normals.length === 15, 'normals: Float32Array(nv * 3)');
  check(m0.uvs instanceof Float32Array && m0.uvs.length === 10, 'uvs: Float32Array(nv * 2)');
  check(m0.indices instanceof Uint32Array && m0.indices.length === 6, 'indices: Uint32Array(nt * 3)');
  const g = gridMesh(4, 2, 4, 2);
  eq(g.positions.length, 15 * 3, '(nx+1)(nz+1) vertices');
  eq(g.indices.length, 16 * 3, '2 nx nz triangles');
  eq(Array.from(g.positions.subarray(0, 6)), [-2, 0, -1, -1, 0, -1], 'first vertices');
  eq(Array.from(g.positions.subarray(14 * 3)), [2, 0, 1], 'last vertex');
  eq(Array.from(g.positions.subarray(7 * 3, 8 * 3)), [0, 0, 0], 'middle vertex at the origin');
  eq(Array.from(g.uvs.subarray(7 * 2, 8 * 2)), [0.5, 0.5], 'uv');
  eq(Array.from(g.indices.subarray(0, 6)), [0, 5, 1, 1, 5, 6], 'first cell');
  eq(Array.from(g.indices.subarray(42)), [8, 13, 9, 9, 13, 14], 'last cell');
  check(Array.from({ length: 15 }, (_, v) => g.normals[v * 3 + 1] === 1 && g.normals[v * 3] === 0 && g.normals[v * 3 + 2] === 0).every(x => x), 'all normals (0, 1, 0)');
  const P = g.positions, I = g.indices;
  let up = true;
  for (let t = 0; t < I.length; t += 3) {
    const a = I[t] * 3, b = I[t + 1] * 3, c = I[t + 2] * 3;
    const ny = (P[b + 2] - P[a + 2]) * (P[c] - P[a]) - (P[b] - P[a]) * (P[c + 2] - P[a + 2]);
    if (!(ny > 0)) up = false;
  }
  check(up, 'every triangle faces +y (counterclockwise from above)');
  const big = gridMesh(10, 10, 500, 400);
  eq(big.indices.length, 500 * 400 * 6, 'big grid triangle count');
  eq(big.positions.length, 501 * 401 * 3, 'big grid vertex count');
}
=== exercise mesh-normals
title: 2. Smooth and flat normals
continues: mesh-grid
--- prompt
Write:

- `computeNormals(mesh)`: area-weighted smooth normals, in place, returning the mesh. Zero `mesh.normals`; for every triangle compute the **unnormalized** cross product `(p1 − p0) × (p2 − p0)` and add it to the normals of its three vertices; finally normalize every vertex normal (leave zero-length ones at zero).
- `flatShade(mesh)`: a new mesh with three vertices per triangle (`nv = 3 * nt`), triangle `t` using vertices `3t, 3t+1, 3t+2` in the same corner order, positions and uvs copied from the corners, and all three normals equal to the triangle's unit face normal.

Both are single passes over flat arrays with scalar locals. **Avoid** looping over all triangles for each vertex (O(V·T)): the timing test computes normals for 720,000 triangles.
--- stubs
function computeNormals(mesh) {
  return mesh;
}

function flatShade(mesh) {
  return mesh;
}
--- solution
// ---- From earlier chapters: seeded PRNG and 3D Perlin noise + fBm (p2-noise) ----
function mulberry32(seed) {
  let a = seed >>> 0;
  return function () {
    a = (a + 0x6D2B79F5) >>> 0;
    let t = a;
    t = Math.imul(t ^ (t >>> 15), t | 1);
    t ^= t + Math.imul(t ^ (t >>> 7), t | 61);
    return ((t ^ (t >>> 14)) >>> 0) / 4294967296;
  };
}
// Improved Perlin noise (Perlin 2002) with a seeded permutation table. Returns roughly [-1, 1].
function makePerlin3(seed) {
  const rng = mulberry32(seed), p = new Uint8Array(512), perm = new Uint8Array(256);
  for (let i = 0; i < 256; i++) perm[i] = i;
  for (let i = 255; i > 0; i--) { const j = (rng() * (i + 1)) | 0, t = perm[i]; perm[i] = perm[j]; perm[j] = t; }
  for (let i = 0; i < 512; i++) p[i] = perm[i & 255];
  const fade = t => t * t * t * (t * (t * 6 - 15) + 10);
  const lerp = (a, b, t) => a + t * (b - a);
  const grad = (h, x, y, z) => {
    h &= 15;
    const u = h < 8 ? x : y, v = h < 4 ? y : (h === 12 || h === 14 ? x : z);
    return ((h & 1) ? -u : u) + ((h & 2) ? -v : v);
  };
  return function (x, y, z) {
    const X = Math.floor(x), Y = Math.floor(y), Z = Math.floor(z);
    x -= X; y -= Y; z -= Z;
    const xi = X & 255, yi = Y & 255, zi = Z & 255;
    const u = fade(x), v = fade(y), w = fade(z);
    const A = p[xi] + yi, AA = p[A] + zi, AB = p[A + 1] + zi, B = p[xi + 1] + yi, BA = p[B] + zi, BB = p[B + 1] + zi;
    return lerp(
      lerp(lerp(grad(p[AA], x, y, z), grad(p[BA], x - 1, y, z), u), lerp(grad(p[AB], x, y - 1, z), grad(p[BB], x - 1, y - 1, z), u), v),
      lerp(lerp(grad(p[AA + 1], x, y, z - 1), grad(p[BA + 1], x - 1, y, z - 1), u), lerp(grad(p[AB + 1], x, y - 1, z - 1), grad(p[BB + 1], x - 1, y - 1, z - 1), u), v),
      w);
  };
}
function fbm3(noise, x, y, z, octaves = 5, lacunarity = 2, gain = 0.5) {
  let sum = 0, amp = 1, freq = 1;
  for (let o = 0; o < octaves; o++) {
    sum += amp * noise(x * freq, y * freq, z * freq);
    freq *= lacunarity; amp *= gain;
  }
  return sum;
}

// Indexed triangle mesh: every array allocated once at its final size.
function createMesh(nv, nt) {
  return {
    positions: new Float32Array(nv * 3),
    normals: new Float32Array(nv * 3),
    uvs: new Float32Array(nv * 2),
    indices: new Uint32Array(nt * 3),
  };
}

function gridMesh(w, d, nx, nz) {
  const m = createMesh((nx + 1) * (nz + 1), nx * nz * 2);
  const { positions: P, normals: N, uvs: U, indices: I } = m;
  for (let j = 0, v = 0; j <= nz; j++) {
    for (let i = 0; i <= nx; i++, v++) {
      P[v * 3] = -w / 2 + w * i / nx; P[v * 3 + 1] = 0; P[v * 3 + 2] = -d / 2 + d * j / nz;
      N[v * 3 + 1] = 1;
      U[v * 2] = i / nx; U[v * 2 + 1] = j / nz;
    }
  }
  for (let j = 0, k = 0; j < nz; j++) {
    for (let i = 0; i < nx; i++) {
      const a = j * (nx + 1) + i, b = a + 1, c = a + nx + 1, e = c + 1;
      I[k++] = a; I[k++] = c; I[k++] = b;
      I[k++] = b; I[k++] = c; I[k++] = e;
    }
  }
  return m;
}

// Smooth normals: sum the unnormalized face normals (length = 2 x area) into each corner, then normalize.
function computeNormals(mesh) {
  const P = mesh.positions, I = mesh.indices, N = mesh.normals;
  N.fill(0);
  for (let t = 0; t < I.length; t += 3) {
    const a = I[t] * 3, b = I[t + 1] * 3, c = I[t + 2] * 3;
    const ux = P[b] - P[a], uy = P[b + 1] - P[a + 1], uz = P[b + 2] - P[a + 2];
    const vx = P[c] - P[a], vy = P[c + 1] - P[a + 1], vz = P[c + 2] - P[a + 2];
    const nx = uy * vz - uz * vy, ny = uz * vx - ux * vz, nz = ux * vy - uy * vx;
    N[a] += nx; N[a + 1] += ny; N[a + 2] += nz;
    N[b] += nx; N[b + 1] += ny; N[b + 2] += nz;
    N[c] += nx; N[c + 1] += ny; N[c + 2] += nz;
  }
  for (let i = 0; i < N.length; i += 3) {
    const l = Math.hypot(N[i], N[i + 1], N[i + 2]);
    if (l > 0) { N[i] /= l; N[i + 1] /= l; N[i + 2] /= l; }
  }
  return mesh;
}

// Flat shading: every triangle gets its own three vertices carrying the face normal.
function flatShade(mesh) {
  const I = mesh.indices, P = mesh.positions, U = mesh.uvs, nt = I.length / 3;
  const m = createMesh(nt * 3, nt);
  const P2 = m.positions, N2 = m.normals, U2 = m.uvs;
  for (let t = 0; t < nt; t++) {
    for (let k = 0; k < 3; k++) {
      const s = I[t * 3 + k], d = t * 3 + k;
      P2[d * 3] = P[s * 3]; P2[d * 3 + 1] = P[s * 3 + 1]; P2[d * 3 + 2] = P[s * 3 + 2];
      U2[d * 2] = U[s * 2]; U2[d * 2 + 1] = U[s * 2 + 1];
      m.indices[d] = d;
    }
    const a = t * 9;
    const ux = P2[a + 3] - P2[a], uy = P2[a + 4] - P2[a + 1], uz = P2[a + 5] - P2[a + 2];
    const vx = P2[a + 6] - P2[a], vy = P2[a + 7] - P2[a + 1], vz = P2[a + 8] - P2[a + 2];
    let nx = uy * vz - uz * vy, ny = uz * vx - ux * vz, nz = ux * vy - uy * vx;
    const l = Math.hypot(nx, ny, nz) || 1;
    nx /= l; ny /= l; nz /= l;
    for (let k = 0; k < 9; k += 3) { N2[a + k] = nx; N2[a + k + 1] = ny; N2[a + k + 2] = nz; }
  }
  return m;
}
--- hint
With `a = I[t] * 3` etc.: `ux = P[b] - P[a]`, ... and `nx = uy * vz - uz * vy`, `ny = uz * vx - ux * vz`, `nz = ux * vy - uy * vx`.
--- hint
The weighting is automatic: the cross product's length is twice the triangle's area, so don't normalize it before adding.
--- harness
{
  const R = a => Array.from(a, v => Math.round(v * 1e4) / 1e4 + 0);
  const ramp = gridMesh(2, 2, 4, 4);
  for (let i = 0; i < ramp.positions.length; i += 3) ramp.positions[i + 1] = ramp.positions[i] * 0.5;
  check(computeNormals(ramp) === ramp, 'returns the same mesh');
  let ok = true;
  for (let i = 0; i < ramp.normals.length; i += 3) if (Math.abs(ramp.normals[i] + 0.4472) > 1e-3 || Math.abs(ramp.normals[i + 1] - 0.8944) > 1e-3 || Math.abs(ramp.normals[i + 2]) > 1e-6) ok = false;
  check(ok, 'a planar ramp y = x/2 has normal (-1, 2, 0)/sqrt(5) everywhere');
  const m = createMesh(5, 2);
  m.positions.set([0, 0, 0, 4, 0, 0, 0, 4, 0, 0, 0, 1, 0, -1, 0]);   // big triangle in xy, small one in yz
  m.indices.set([0, 1, 2, 0, 3, 4]);
  m.normals.fill(7);
  computeNormals(m);
  eq(R(m.normals.subarray(0, 3)), [0.0624, 0, 0.9981], 'shared vertex: weighted by area (16 vs 1)');
  eq(R(m.normals.subarray(3, 6)), [0, 0, 1], 'corner of the big triangle only');
  const z = createMesh(4, 1); z.indices.set([0, 1, 2]); z.positions.set([0, 0, 0, 1, 0, 0, 0, 1, 0, 9, 9, 9]); z.normals.fill(3);
  computeNormals(z);
  eq(R(z.normals.subarray(9)), [0, 0, 0], 'unused vertex: zero normal');
  const g = gridMesh(2, 2, 1, 1);
  g.positions[3 * 3 + 1] = 1;                    // lift one corner: two faces with different normals
  const f = flatShade(g);
  eq(f.positions.length, 18, 'flat: 3 vertices per triangle');
  eq(Array.from(f.indices), [0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5], 'flat indices');
  eq(R(f.positions.subarray(9, 18)), [1, 0, -1, -1, 0, 1, 1, 1, 1], 'positions copied in corner order');
  eq(R(f.uvs.subarray(6, 12)), [1, 0, 0, 1, 1, 1], 'uvs copied');
  eq(R(f.normals.subarray(0, 9)), [0, 1, 0, 0, 1, 0, 0, 1, 0], 'first face normal');
  eq(R(f.normals.subarray(9, 12)), [-0.4082, 0.8165, -0.4082], 'second face normal (unit)');
  const big = gridMesh(10, 10, 600, 600);
  for (let i = 0; i < big.positions.length; i += 3) big.positions[i + 1] = Math.sin(big.positions[i]) * Math.cos(big.positions[i + 2]);
  const t0 = performance.now();
  computeNormals(big);
  const ms = performance.now() - t0;
  const c = (300 * 601 + 300) * 3;
  near(big.normals[c], -Math.SQRT1_2, 1e-3, 'big grid: normal at the origin, x'); near(big.normals[c + 1], Math.SQRT1_2, 1e-3, 'big grid: normal at the origin, y');
  check(ms < 400, `720,000 triangles took ${ms.toFixed(0)} ms (limit 400)`);
}
=== exercise mesh-render
title: 3. A z-buffered rasterizer
continues: mesh-normals
--- prompt
Now a way to see meshes. The stubs add column-major matrix helpers (`perspective`, `lookAt`, `mul4`, the same layout WebGPU uses, depth 0..1), and a render target: `createTarget(w, h)` → `{ width, height, color: Uint8ClampedArray(w*h*4), depth: Float32Array(w*h) }`, `clearTarget(t, r, g, b)` (depth to `Infinity`) and `blit(ctx, t)`.

Write `renderMesh(target, mesh, viewProj, opts)`. Options: `color` (`[r, g, b]`, default `[200, 200, 200]`), `light` (direction *toward* the light, normalize it, default `[0.4, 0.8, 0.45]`), `ambient` (default 0.2).

1. **Per vertex, once**, into a reusable scratch `Float32Array`: clip = viewProj × (x, y, z, 1). If `w <= 1e-6` mark the vertex unusable. Otherwise screen `sx = (cx/w · 0.5 + 0.5) · width`, `sy = (0.5 − cy/w · 0.5) · height`, depth `cz/w`, and intensity `ambient + (1 − ambient) · max(0, n · l)`.
2. **Per triangle**: skip it if a vertex is unusable. Signed area `(x1−x0)(y2−y0) − (x2−x0)(y1−y0)`; front faces have **negative** area (counterclockwise turns clockwise when y points down); skip area ≥ 0.
3. Loop over the triangle's bounding box clamped to the target. For pixel center `(px + 0.5, py + 0.5)` compute barycentric weights from edge functions divided by the area; skip the pixel if any is negative.
4. Interpolate depth; skip if it's outside 0..1 or not smaller than `depth[p]`. Otherwise store it and write `color · intensity` (intensity interpolated) with alpha 255.

**Avoid** scanning the whole screen per triangle, allocating per triangle or pixel, and transforming a vertex once per triangle that uses it.
--- stubs
// Column-major 4x4 matrices (the layout WebGPU uses), depth range 0..1.
function perspective(fovy, aspect, near, far) {
  const f = 1 / Math.tan(fovy / 2), m = new Float32Array(16);
  m[0] = f / aspect; m[5] = f; m[10] = far / (near - far); m[11] = -1; m[14] = near * far / (near - far);
  return m;
}
function lookAt(eye, target, up) {
  let zx = eye[0] - target[0], zy = eye[1] - target[1], zz = eye[2] - target[2];
  let l = Math.hypot(zx, zy, zz); zx /= l; zy /= l; zz /= l;
  let xx = up[1] * zz - up[2] * zy, xy = up[2] * zx - up[0] * zz, xz = up[0] * zy - up[1] * zx;
  l = Math.hypot(xx, xy, xz); xx /= l; xy /= l; xz /= l;
  const yx = zy * xz - zz * xy, yy = zz * xx - zx * xz, yz = zx * xy - zy * xx;
  return new Float32Array([xx, yx, zx, 0, xy, yy, zy, 0, xz, yz, zz, 0,
    -(xx * eye[0] + xy * eye[1] + xz * eye[2]), -(yx * eye[0] + yy * eye[1] + yz * eye[2]), -(zx * eye[0] + zy * eye[1] + zz * eye[2]), 1]);
}
function mul4(a, b) {   // a * b
  const m = new Float32Array(16);
  for (let c = 0; c < 4; c++) for (let r = 0; r < 4; r++)
    m[c * 4 + r] = a[r] * b[c * 4] + a[4 + r] * b[c * 4 + 1] + a[8 + r] * b[c * 4 + 2] + a[12 + r] * b[c * 4 + 3];
  return m;
}
// A render target: RGBA8 color plus a float depth buffer.
function createTarget(width, height) {
  return { width, height, color: new Uint8ClampedArray(width * height * 4), depth: new Float32Array(width * height) };
}
function clearTarget(t, r, g, b) {
  const c = t.color;
  for (let i = 0; i < c.length; i += 4) { c[i] = r; c[i + 1] = g; c[i + 2] = b; c[i + 3] = 255; }
  t.depth.fill(Infinity);
}
function blit(ctx, t) {
  ctx.putImageData(new ImageData(t.color, t.width, t.height), 0, 0);
}

function renderMesh(target, mesh, viewProj, opts = {}) {
}
--- solution
// ---- From earlier chapters: seeded PRNG and 3D Perlin noise + fBm (p2-noise) ----
function mulberry32(seed) {
  let a = seed >>> 0;
  return function () {
    a = (a + 0x6D2B79F5) >>> 0;
    let t = a;
    t = Math.imul(t ^ (t >>> 15), t | 1);
    t ^= t + Math.imul(t ^ (t >>> 7), t | 61);
    return ((t ^ (t >>> 14)) >>> 0) / 4294967296;
  };
}
// Improved Perlin noise (Perlin 2002) with a seeded permutation table. Returns roughly [-1, 1].
function makePerlin3(seed) {
  const rng = mulberry32(seed), p = new Uint8Array(512), perm = new Uint8Array(256);
  for (let i = 0; i < 256; i++) perm[i] = i;
  for (let i = 255; i > 0; i--) { const j = (rng() * (i + 1)) | 0, t = perm[i]; perm[i] = perm[j]; perm[j] = t; }
  for (let i = 0; i < 512; i++) p[i] = perm[i & 255];
  const fade = t => t * t * t * (t * (t * 6 - 15) + 10);
  const lerp = (a, b, t) => a + t * (b - a);
  const grad = (h, x, y, z) => {
    h &= 15;
    const u = h < 8 ? x : y, v = h < 4 ? y : (h === 12 || h === 14 ? x : z);
    return ((h & 1) ? -u : u) + ((h & 2) ? -v : v);
  };
  return function (x, y, z) {
    const X = Math.floor(x), Y = Math.floor(y), Z = Math.floor(z);
    x -= X; y -= Y; z -= Z;
    const xi = X & 255, yi = Y & 255, zi = Z & 255;
    const u = fade(x), v = fade(y), w = fade(z);
    const A = p[xi] + yi, AA = p[A] + zi, AB = p[A + 1] + zi, B = p[xi + 1] + yi, BA = p[B] + zi, BB = p[B + 1] + zi;
    return lerp(
      lerp(lerp(grad(p[AA], x, y, z), grad(p[BA], x - 1, y, z), u), lerp(grad(p[AB], x, y - 1, z), grad(p[BB], x - 1, y - 1, z), u), v),
      lerp(lerp(grad(p[AA + 1], x, y, z - 1), grad(p[BA + 1], x - 1, y, z - 1), u), lerp(grad(p[AB + 1], x, y - 1, z - 1), grad(p[BB + 1], x - 1, y - 1, z - 1), u), v),
      w);
  };
}
function fbm3(noise, x, y, z, octaves = 5, lacunarity = 2, gain = 0.5) {
  let sum = 0, amp = 1, freq = 1;
  for (let o = 0; o < octaves; o++) {
    sum += amp * noise(x * freq, y * freq, z * freq);
    freq *= lacunarity; amp *= gain;
  }
  return sum;
}

// Indexed triangle mesh: every array allocated once at its final size.
function createMesh(nv, nt) {
  return {
    positions: new Float32Array(nv * 3),
    normals: new Float32Array(nv * 3),
    uvs: new Float32Array(nv * 2),
    indices: new Uint32Array(nt * 3),
  };
}

function gridMesh(w, d, nx, nz) {
  const m = createMesh((nx + 1) * (nz + 1), nx * nz * 2);
  const { positions: P, normals: N, uvs: U, indices: I } = m;
  for (let j = 0, v = 0; j <= nz; j++) {
    for (let i = 0; i <= nx; i++, v++) {
      P[v * 3] = -w / 2 + w * i / nx; P[v * 3 + 1] = 0; P[v * 3 + 2] = -d / 2 + d * j / nz;
      N[v * 3 + 1] = 1;
      U[v * 2] = i / nx; U[v * 2 + 1] = j / nz;
    }
  }
  for (let j = 0, k = 0; j < nz; j++) {
    for (let i = 0; i < nx; i++) {
      const a = j * (nx + 1) + i, b = a + 1, c = a + nx + 1, e = c + 1;
      I[k++] = a; I[k++] = c; I[k++] = b;
      I[k++] = b; I[k++] = c; I[k++] = e;
    }
  }
  return m;
}

// Smooth normals: sum the unnormalized face normals (length = 2 x area) into each corner, then normalize.
function computeNormals(mesh) {
  const P = mesh.positions, I = mesh.indices, N = mesh.normals;
  N.fill(0);
  for (let t = 0; t < I.length; t += 3) {
    const a = I[t] * 3, b = I[t + 1] * 3, c = I[t + 2] * 3;
    const ux = P[b] - P[a], uy = P[b + 1] - P[a + 1], uz = P[b + 2] - P[a + 2];
    const vx = P[c] - P[a], vy = P[c + 1] - P[a + 1], vz = P[c + 2] - P[a + 2];
    const nx = uy * vz - uz * vy, ny = uz * vx - ux * vz, nz = ux * vy - uy * vx;
    N[a] += nx; N[a + 1] += ny; N[a + 2] += nz;
    N[b] += nx; N[b + 1] += ny; N[b + 2] += nz;
    N[c] += nx; N[c + 1] += ny; N[c + 2] += nz;
  }
  for (let i = 0; i < N.length; i += 3) {
    const l = Math.hypot(N[i], N[i + 1], N[i + 2]);
    if (l > 0) { N[i] /= l; N[i + 1] /= l; N[i + 2] /= l; }
  }
  return mesh;
}

// Flat shading: every triangle gets its own three vertices carrying the face normal.
function flatShade(mesh) {
  const I = mesh.indices, P = mesh.positions, U = mesh.uvs, nt = I.length / 3;
  const m = createMesh(nt * 3, nt);
  const P2 = m.positions, N2 = m.normals, U2 = m.uvs;
  for (let t = 0; t < nt; t++) {
    for (let k = 0; k < 3; k++) {
      const s = I[t * 3 + k], d = t * 3 + k;
      P2[d * 3] = P[s * 3]; P2[d * 3 + 1] = P[s * 3 + 1]; P2[d * 3 + 2] = P[s * 3 + 2];
      U2[d * 2] = U[s * 2]; U2[d * 2 + 1] = U[s * 2 + 1];
      m.indices[d] = d;
    }
    const a = t * 9;
    const ux = P2[a + 3] - P2[a], uy = P2[a + 4] - P2[a + 1], uz = P2[a + 5] - P2[a + 2];
    const vx = P2[a + 6] - P2[a], vy = P2[a + 7] - P2[a + 1], vz = P2[a + 8] - P2[a + 2];
    let nx = uy * vz - uz * vy, ny = uz * vx - ux * vz, nz = ux * vy - uy * vx;
    const l = Math.hypot(nx, ny, nz) || 1;
    nx /= l; ny /= l; nz /= l;
    for (let k = 0; k < 9; k += 3) { N2[a + k] = nx; N2[a + k + 1] = ny; N2[a + k + 2] = nz; }
  }
  return m;
}

// Column-major 4x4 matrices (the layout WebGPU uses), depth range 0..1.
function perspective(fovy, aspect, near, far) {
  const f = 1 / Math.tan(fovy / 2), m = new Float32Array(16);
  m[0] = f / aspect; m[5] = f; m[10] = far / (near - far); m[11] = -1; m[14] = near * far / (near - far);
  return m;
}
function lookAt(eye, target, up) {
  let zx = eye[0] - target[0], zy = eye[1] - target[1], zz = eye[2] - target[2];
  let l = Math.hypot(zx, zy, zz); zx /= l; zy /= l; zz /= l;
  let xx = up[1] * zz - up[2] * zy, xy = up[2] * zx - up[0] * zz, xz = up[0] * zy - up[1] * zx;
  l = Math.hypot(xx, xy, xz); xx /= l; xy /= l; xz /= l;
  const yx = zy * xz - zz * xy, yy = zz * xx - zx * xz, yz = zx * xy - zy * xx;
  return new Float32Array([xx, yx, zx, 0, xy, yy, zy, 0, xz, yz, zz, 0,
    -(xx * eye[0] + xy * eye[1] + xz * eye[2]), -(yx * eye[0] + yy * eye[1] + yz * eye[2]), -(zx * eye[0] + zy * eye[1] + zz * eye[2]), 1]);
}
function mul4(a, b) {   // a * b
  const m = new Float32Array(16);
  for (let c = 0; c < 4; c++) for (let r = 0; r < 4; r++)
    m[c * 4 + r] = a[r] * b[c * 4] + a[4 + r] * b[c * 4 + 1] + a[8 + r] * b[c * 4 + 2] + a[12 + r] * b[c * 4 + 3];
  return m;
}
// A render target: RGBA8 color plus a float depth buffer.
function createTarget(width, height) {
  return { width, height, color: new Uint8ClampedArray(width * height * 4), depth: new Float32Array(width * height) };
}
function clearTarget(t, r, g, b) {
  const c = t.color;
  for (let i = 0; i < c.length; i += 4) { c[i] = r; c[i + 1] = g; c[i + 2] = b; c[i + 3] = 255; }
  t.depth.fill(Infinity);
}
function blit(ctx, t) {
  ctx.putImageData(new ImageData(t.color, t.width, t.height), 0, 0);
}

// Z-buffered, back-face-culled, Gouraud-shaded (per-vertex Lambert) rasterizer.
let scratch = new Float32Array(0);
function renderMesh(target, mesh, viewProj, opts = {}) {
  const color = opts.color ?? [200, 200, 200], ambient = opts.ambient ?? 0.2;
  let [lx, ly, lz] = opts.light ?? [0.4, 0.8, 0.45];
  const ll = Math.hypot(lx, ly, lz); lx /= ll; ly /= ll; lz /= ll;
  const P = mesh.positions, N = mesh.normals, I = mesh.indices, nv = P.length / 3;
  const W = target.width, H = target.height, C = target.color, Z = target.depth, m = viewProj;
  if (scratch.length < nv * 4) scratch = new Float32Array(nv * 4);
  const S = scratch;   // per vertex: screen x, screen y, depth, intensity (w <= 0 marks it unusable)
  for (let v = 0; v < nv; v++) {
    const x = P[v * 3], y = P[v * 3 + 1], z = P[v * 3 + 2];
    const cx = m[0] * x + m[4] * y + m[8] * z + m[12], cy = m[1] * x + m[5] * y + m[9] * z + m[13];
    const cz = m[2] * x + m[6] * y + m[10] * z + m[14], cw = m[3] * x + m[7] * y + m[11] * z + m[15];
    const d = N[v * 3] * lx + N[v * 3 + 1] * ly + N[v * 3 + 2] * lz;
    S[v * 4 + 3] = ambient + (1 - ambient) * (d > 0 ? d : 0);
    if (cw <= 1e-6) { S[v * 4 + 2] = NaN; continue; }
    S[v * 4] = (cx / cw * 0.5 + 0.5) * W;
    S[v * 4 + 1] = (0.5 - cy / cw * 0.5) * H;
    S[v * 4 + 2] = cz / cw;
  }
  const [cr, cg, cb] = color;
  for (let t = 0; t < I.length; t += 3) {
    const a = I[t] * 4, b = I[t + 1] * 4, c = I[t + 2] * 4;
    const x0 = S[a], y0 = S[a + 1], z0 = S[a + 2], x1 = S[b], y1 = S[b + 1], z1 = S[b + 2], x2 = S[c], y2 = S[c + 1], z2 = S[c + 2];
    if (z0 !== z0 || z1 !== z1 || z2 !== z2) continue;              // behind the camera
    const area = (x1 - x0) * (y2 - y0) - (x2 - x0) * (y1 - y0);
    if (area >= 0) continue;                                          // back face (y is down)
    let minX = Math.max(0, Math.floor(Math.min(x0, x1, x2))), maxX = Math.min(W - 1, Math.ceil(Math.max(x0, x1, x2)));
    let minY = Math.max(0, Math.floor(Math.min(y0, y1, y2))), maxY = Math.min(H - 1, Math.ceil(Math.max(y0, y1, y2)));
    const i0 = S[a + 3], i1 = S[b + 3], i2 = S[c + 3], inv = 1 / area;
    for (let py = minY; py <= maxY; py++) {
      const sy = py + 0.5;
      for (let px = minX; px <= maxX; px++) {
        const sx = px + 0.5;
        const w0 = ((x2 - x1) * (sy - y1) - (y2 - y1) * (sx - x1)) * inv;
        const w1 = ((x0 - x2) * (sy - y2) - (y0 - y2) * (sx - x2)) * inv;
        const w2 = 1 - w0 - w1;
        if (w0 < 0 || w1 < 0 || w2 < 0) continue;
        const z = w0 * z0 + w1 * z1 + w2 * z2, p = py * W + px;
        if (z >= Z[p] || z < 0 || z > 1) continue;
        Z[p] = z;
        const k = w0 * i0 + w1 * i1 + w2 * i2, o = p * 4;
        C[o] = cr * k; C[o + 1] = cg * k; C[o + 2] = cb * k; C[o + 3] = 255;
      }
    }
  }
}
--- hint
Barycentric weight of vertex 0 for pixel (sx, sy): `w0 = ((x2 - x1) * (sy - y1) - (y2 - y1) * (sx - x1)) / area`, similarly `w1` with the edge from 2 to 0, and `w2 = 1 - w0 - w1`.
--- hint
Look at your grid by adding: `const t = createTarget(canvas.width, canvas.height); clearTarget(t, 20, 24, 30); const vp = mul4(perspective(0.9, 1, 0.1, 100), lookAt([3, 3, 4], [0, 0, 0], [0, 1, 0])); const g = gridMesh(3, 3, 30, 30); for (let i = 0; i < g.positions.length; i += 3) g.positions[i + 1] = 0.3 * Math.sin(3 * g.positions[i]); renderMesh(t, computeNormals(g), vp, { color: [90, 170, 220] }); blit(canvas.getContext('2d'), t);`
--- harness
{
  const S = 32, vpTop = mul4(perspective(0.8, 1, 0.1, 100), lookAt([0, 4, 0], [0, 0, 0], [0, 0, -1]));
  const px = (t, x, y) => Array.from(t.color.subarray((y * t.width + x) * 4, (y * t.width + x) * 4 + 4));
  let t = createTarget(S, S); clearTarget(t, 10, 20, 30);
  renderMesh(t, gridMesh(2, 2, 1, 1), vpTop, { color: [200, 100, 50], light: [0, 1, 0] });
  eq(px(t, 16, 16), [200, 100, 50, 255], 'center pixel: full Lambert from straight above');
  eq(px(t, 1, 1), [10, 20, 30, 255], 'outside the quad: background');
  check(t.depth[16 * S + 16] > 0 && t.depth[16 * S + 16] < 1, 'depth written in 0..1');
  eq(t.depth[1 * S + 1], Infinity, 'depth untouched outside');
  let covered = 0; for (let i = 0; i < S * S; i++) if (t.depth[i] < 1) covered++;
  check(covered > 280 && covered < 340, `quad covers about 311 pixels (got ${covered})`);
  t = createTarget(S, S); clearTarget(t, 0, 0, 0);
  renderMesh(t, gridMesh(2, 2, 1, 1), vpTop, { color: [100, 100, 100], light: [1, 0, 0], ambient: 0.25 });
  eq(px(t, 16, 16), [25, 25, 25, 255], 'light from the side: ambient only');
  const g = gridMesh(2, 2, 1, 1), flipped = gridMesh(2, 2, 1, 1);
  for (let i = 0; i < flipped.indices.length; i += 3) { const k = flipped.indices[i + 1]; flipped.indices[i + 1] = flipped.indices[i + 2]; flipped.indices[i + 2] = k; }
  t = createTarget(S, S); clearTarget(t, 0, 0, 0);
  renderMesh(t, flipped, vpTop, { light: [0, 1, 0] });
  eq(px(t, 16, 16), [0, 0, 0, 255], 'back faces are culled');
  const high = gridMesh(1, 1, 1, 1);
  for (let i = 0; i < high.positions.length; i += 3) high.positions[i + 1] = 1;
  for (const order of [[g, high], [high, g]]) {
    t = createTarget(S, S); clearTarget(t, 0, 0, 0);
    renderMesh(t, order[0], vpTop, { color: order[0] === g ? [255, 0, 0] : [0, 0, 255], light: [0, 1, 0] });
    renderMesh(t, order[1], vpTop, { color: order[1] === g ? [255, 0, 0] : [0, 0, 255], light: [0, 1, 0] });
    eq(px(t, 16, 16), [0, 0, 255, 255], 'z-buffer: the nearer quad wins in either order');
    eq(px(t, 8, 16), [255, 0, 0, 255], 'z-buffer: the far quad shows where the near one is absent');
  }
  t = createTarget(S, S); clearTarget(t, 0, 0, 0);
  renderMesh(t, gridMesh(200, 200, 4, 4), vpTop, { light: [0, 1, 0] });
  let all = true; for (let i = 0; i < S * S; i++) if (t.color[i * 4] !== 200) all = false;
  check(all, 'triangles larger than the screen are clipped to it and fill every pixel');
  t = createTarget(S, S); clearTarget(t, 0, 0, 0);
  const behind = mul4(perspective(0.8, 1, 0.1, 100), lookAt([0, 4, 0], [0, 8, 0], [0, 0, -1]));
  renderMesh(t, gridMesh(2, 2, 1, 1), behind, { light: [0, 1, 0] });
  eq(px(t, 16, 16), [0, 0, 0, 255], 'geometry behind the camera is skipped');
  const big = gridMesh(2, 2, 300, 300);
  for (let i = 0; i < big.positions.length; i += 3) big.positions[i + 1] = 0.2 * Math.sin(5 * big.positions[i]);
  computeNormals(big);
  t = createTarget(256, 256); clearTarget(t, 0, 0, 0);
  const t0 = performance.now();
  renderMesh(t, big, mul4(perspective(0.9, 1, 0.1, 100), lookAt([2, 2.5, 2.5], [0, 0, 0], [0, 1, 0])), {});
  const ms = performance.now() - t0;
  let lit = 0; for (let i = 0; i < 256 * 256; i++) if (t.color[i * 4] > 0) lit++;
  check(lit > 8000, 'big mesh drawn');
  check(ms < 1000, `180,000 triangles took ${ms.toFixed(0)} ms (limit 1000): rasterize bounding boxes only`);
